# Decision Index

Decisions live inside long documents under eight identifier namespaces with no
index (`D*`, `A*`, `R-*`, `S*`, `C.*`, `E.*`, `M*`, `DEC-*`), and production
modules used to cite section numbers of a spec since superseded. Rules are
versionless; section numbers are not. This file is the index for the four
namespaces that carry live, enforced decisions: `D*` (topology), `A*` (audit
amendments), `R-*` (guardrail rules), `DEC-*` (architecture). `S*`, `C.*`,
`E.*`, `M*` are structural/procedural section labels inside
`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-03-nora-strands-spec.md`, not decisions in
their own right, and are not indexed here.

See `ARCHITECTURE.md` for the map this index supports, and each identifier's
defining document for the full rationale — this file is deliberately terse.

## D — Topology (`design/00-overview.md`)

| ID | Statement | Enforcing test |
|---|---|---|
| D1 | Ambient ingest never passes through the Agent SDK; the SDK is constructed, invoked, and discarded once per tag. **Amended by A3** (see below): the ambient path may later host a lightweight relevance classifier, still not the full Agent SDK. | `test_ambient_never_constructs_agent` (R-1) |
| D2 | SQLite is an audit record, not the semantic store; Honcho is the semantic store (peer cards, relationships, durable recall). | no dedicated test — a schema-level property |
| D3 | The Strands Agents SDK is the tagging-path engine — no hand-rolled loop, context builder, or session store. | `test_strands_import_boundary` (structural: only `nora/agent/**` may import `strands`) |
| D4 | Every tag is a fresh, stateless agent invocation; continuity comes from SQLite + Honcho pre-filled into context, never SDK session persistence. **Amended by DEC-3**: the injection invariant holds, but an additive, TTL'd `Episode` concept is accepted for later — not implemented in stage 1. | no dedicated test (statelessness itself); `tests/integration/test_statelessness.py` exercises the invariant |
| D5 | Everything identity-related (trigger text, account, peer mapping, model, endpoints) is config, never hardcoded. | `tests/test_config.py` (`extra="forbid"`, no hardcoded identity fields) |
| D6 | Peer mapping is manual, one operator-maintained file — no service, no UI. **Amended by A2**: two-level mapping (`person_id` → name + per-platform sender IDs), one person = one Honcho peer across platforms. **`design/04`'s identity-discovery mechanism is further amended by DEC-9**: identity discovery moves onto the `PlatformAdapter` port (`resolve_identity()`), not read from a private adapter attribute. | `test_self_tag_ignored` (R-12, depends on identity resolution); `tests/test_persons.py` | **Further amended by DEC-14/DEC-16 (Phase 8)**: the file now maps one person to many ACCOUNTS per platform, not one id per platform; and while it stays hand-edited — Nora never writes it — adoption is now *lazy*, so an edit plus a restart backfills that person's pre-mapping messages into Honcho rather than leaving a permanent hole. `nora persons pending` only ever suggests; it writes nothing.

## A — Audit amendments (`design/00-05*.md`)

Numbered by the 2026-08-03 audit pass. A7 does not appear anywhere in the
design corpus — not an omission here, just never assigned.

| ID | Statement | Defining doc | Enforcing test |
|---|---|---|---|
| A1 | Platform abstraction seam (`PlatformAdapter`) so new platforms are extensions, not rebuilds. | `design/00-overview.md` §Platform Abstraction, `design/01-ambient-ingest.md` | `tests/contract/test_port_conformance.py::test_signal_adapter_satisfies_platform_adapter` — `isinstance(SignalAdapter(...), PlatformAdapter)` is `True`; `nora.platform.registry.AdapterRegistry` is the seam a second platform actually extends |
| A2 | Two-level peer mapping: `persons:` (`person_id` → name + per-platform sender IDs); one person = one Honcho peer across platforms. Amends D6. | `design/00-overview.md`, `design/04-config-identity.md` §Peer Mapping | `tests/test_persons.py` |
| A3 | The ambient path may later host a lightweight relevance classifier (a single cheap completion, not the Agent SDK) replacing the boolean trigger gate. Amends D1 in principle; deferred in practice — a future milestone, `CompositePolicy([TriggerTextPolicy, ClassifierPolicy])` per DEC-1's staging (spec §7). | `design/00-overview.md`, `design/02-tagging-path.md` §True Ambient Hooks | not built — no test |
| A4 | Runtime isolation: dedicated system user / container, restricted egress to the three localhost endpoints, filesystem writes limited to `data/`, process supervision with auto-restart. | `design/01-ambient-ingest.md` §Environment & Isolation | none dedicated (deployment-level: `Dockerfile`, `docker-compose.yml`) |
| A5 | OTel tracing enabled by default — a config toggle, not a build flag. | `design/01-ambient-ingest.md`, `design/03-strands-reuse.md` | `tests/test_tracing.py` |
| A6 | Structured logs with per-tag correlation ID; non-optional `GET /health`; a metrics endpoint. | `design/01-ambient-ingest.md` §Observability | `tests/test_health.py`, `tests/test_logging.py` |
| A8 | Silent-failure rate (tags with no reply / total tags, target <1%) is the observability escape hatch for the silent-failure doctrine (R-5) — it makes "invisible when broken" measurable. | `design/05-evaluation.md` | metric only (`nora_silent_failures_total`); no assertion test |
| A9 | Record-first, send-after: the reply is audited (`is_nora=1`, `delivery_status=pending`) *before* `send_reply` runs, so a failed send never loses the reply from context. | `design/02-tagging-path.md` §4 | exercised by `test_silent_failure_matrix` (R-5) and `tests/test_guards_deliverer.py` |
| A10 | `strands-agents` is pinned in `pyproject.toml`; upgrades are manual and gated on the smoke test. | `design/03-strands-reuse.md` | none dedicated (a `pyproject.toml` constraint) |
| A11 | Tool-use policy: the pre-fill header states what context is already present, so the model doesn't burn a roundtrip on redundant tool calls. Feeds R-15. | `design/02-tagging-path.md`, `design/05-evaluation.md` | `test_header_states_what_is_already_present`, `test_no_redundant_read_fails_when_prefill_was_sufficient` (R-15) |
| A12 | CI fast gate (every change) runs deterministic evaluators + parser/db/tag unit tests + latency smoke. | `design/05-evaluation.md` | CI config itself (`.github/workflows/gates.yml`), not a single test |

## R-1…R-17 — Enforced guardrail rules

Source of truth for the rule statements and the assigned test names:
`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-03-nora-strands-spec.md` §B.1. The registry of
which named test satisfies which rule is `tests/test_acceptance_rules.py::RULE_TESTS`
— that dict, not this table, is what `test_every_enforced_rule_has_a_named_test`
checks against; this table is sourced from it, not the reverse.

| ID | Statement | Enforcing test |
|---|---|---|
| R-1 | **Only the tagging path and the heartbeat's fire path may cause a send; ambient traffic never reaches the agent.** *Revised 2026-08-08 (DEC-69). The old wording, preserved: “Only the tagging path may cause a send; ambient traffic never reaches the agent.”* The second clause is untouched and is what this row's test still enforces. The first changed because `nora.app.fire.FirePath` is a send **not caused by a tag** — Nora's first unprompted-speech path. What did NOT change is that the set of senders is closed and enumerated: it went from one to two, by decision, and a third is a rule change rather than a new module. | `test_ambient_never_constructs_agent` |
| R-1b | **The tag path and the fire path are the only two senders, structurally.** R-1's revised first clause needed its own test and could not reuse R-1's: `test_ambient_never_constructs_agent` asserts both clauses but harnesses only AMBIENT input, so a reminders fire path never enters it and it stayed green while the rule changed underneath it — amending the row alone would have deleted the only written brake on a third send path with nothing going red. Three AST facts, one per link in the chain: `Deliverer` is the only caller of `adapter.send` (so R-2's latch, R-4, R-6's leak scan, R-18's egress scan and A9's audit-first write cannot be bypassed), `Deliverer` is constructed only by `deliver.build` (where `audience` has no default), and `deliver.build` has exactly two callers. | `test_only_the_tag_path_and_the_fire_path_can_send` |
| R-2 | **One tag produces one turn, and a turn produces at most `NATIVE_TOOLS["send_reply"].budget` messages, all to the tagging conversation.** *Revised 2026-08-07 (DEC-40). The old wording, preserved: “`send_reply` executes at most once per tag, counting attempts, not successes.”* The invariant was never the number one — it is that a tag cannot produce unbounded output and cannot produce output anywhere but where it was tagged. The second half is untouched (`send_reply` still goes to the deliverer's bound target — DEC-21, R-3), and “counting attempts, not successes” is untouched: a failed send still spends a message, because there is no auto-retry. What changed is that the bound is a NUMBER read from the tool table rather than the literal 1, and **that budget is still 1 as shipped**, so behaviour is byte-identical until an operator raises it. | `test_second_send_reply_is_cancelled` |
| R-2b | The `Deliverer` latch is bounded by `message_cap`, independent of `ReplyGuard` — and `message_cap` is the SAME number, read out of the same `ToolRegistry.budgets()` mapping by `nora.engagement.stages.deliver.build`. *Revised 2026-08-07 (DEC-40); previously “single-shot”, which at a cap of 1 is the same sentence.* | `test_deliverer_latch_is_single_shot` |
| R-3 | `send_reply` may only target the conversation that produced the tag. **Mechanism changed 2026-08-05 (DEC-21): held BY CONSTRUCTION, not by veto.** Both scoped tools ignore the model's `conversation_id` — `read_buffer` reads the closure value (Task 1's cross-group leak fix) and `send_reply` calls `deliverer.deliver(text)`, whose `Deliverer` binds its target at construction — so the argument is inert and nothing the model writes can change where a message goes. That is strictly stronger than the old check, which could be satisfied by a model echoing the right string. A mismatch is counted as `nora_tool_scope_violations_total{tool}`, not vetoed. **Mechanism narrowed again 2026-08-06 (DEC-24): the argument no longer exists.** No tool declares a conversation parameter, so the model is never asked for one; the counter now fires only when a model supplies an OUT-OF-SCHEMA `conversation_id` (which Strands drops during validation), on any tool, and never on a well-formed call. | `test_send_to_other_group_cannot_escape_the_tagged_conversation` |
| R-4 | Empty or whitespace-only reply text is never delivered. | `test_empty_reply_not_sent` |
| R-5 | No error text ever reaches the group — silent failure, always. | `test_silent_failure_matrix` |
| R-6 | No reply may contain the canary token or a verbatim ≥40-character span of the assembled doctrine text. | `test_leak_guard_blocks_canary` |
| R-6b | ...and a verbatim doctrine span specifically. | `test_leak_guard_blocks_doctrine_verbatim` |
| R-6c | A normal, non-leaking reply is never blocked by the leak guard. | `test_leak_guard_allows_normal_reply` |
| R-7 | Per-tag tool-call budget (`read_buffer` ≤ 2, `read_peer_card` ≤ 1, `send_reply` ≤ 1); unknown tools default-deny. **DEC-10** [built, agent/tool-registry phase]: the budget table is now injected — `ReplyGuard(budgets=...)` reads `nora.agent.registry.ToolRegistry.budgets()`, threaded through `nora.engagement.stages.deliver.build` — instead of a hardcoded `ReplyGuard.BUDGETS` class attribute. Unknown-tool default-deny (the other half of this rule) is unchanged: a tool absent from `budgets` is still vetoed. | `test_tool_call_budget_enforced_for_read_buffer` (budget half); `test_an_unknown_tool_is_cancelled` (default-deny half, `tests/test_guards_replyguard.py`) |
| R-8 | Hard invocation ceiling per tag (`turns`, `output_tokens`, `total_tokens`). **All three are LOOP budgets, and all three were wrong until 2026-08-06 — see R-24 for the measurements and R-23 for why nobody could tell.** `output_tokens` in particular is not the provider's per-call ceiling (`llm.max_tokens`) and used to literally be it. A cap absent from the dict is not a cap: Strands' `_check_limits` skips one it cannot find, which is why `nora.config.LimitsConfig` makes all three required. | `test_limits_passed_through`; `test_no_single_call_can_spend_the_whole_turn_budget` (R-24) |
| R-9 | Debounce: a second tag for the same conversation within `min_interval_seconds` is dropped. | `test_debounce_window` |
| R-10 | In-flight lock: at most one tag per conversation; released on every exit path including timeout/error. | `test_concurrent_tags_same_conversation` |
| R-10b | ...released specifically on timeout. | `test_lock_released_on_timeout` |
| R-10c | ...released specifically on an LLM error. | `test_lock_released_on_llm_error` |
| R-11 | Trigger validation — only genuine trigger text fires (word-boundary, code-span-aware, Unicode-normalized). | `test_trigger_matrix` |
| R-12 | Nora never triggers herself — `is_nora=1` rows and her own `sender_id` can never fire the trigger. **DEC-9** moves the identity source from a private adapter attribute (`adapter._account`) to `resolve_identity()` on the port, so this becomes platform-generic instead of Signal-specific. **Built in the platforms phase**: `nora.engagement.stages.admission.run` checks `message.sender_id in identity.aliases` (membership, not equality against a single id — signal-cli sometimes pairs a phone number with a UUID for one account); the `_AccountHolder` structural shim that read `adapter._account` directly is deleted. | `test_self_tag_ignored` |
| R-13 | All group-derived text is XML-escaped and enclosed in labelled untrusted blocks. | `test_prefill_escapes_angle_brackets` |
| R-13b | ...even a forged closing tag inside the text. | `test_forged_closing_tag_is_escaped` |
| R-14 | Prompt-injection behaviour (never reveal/paraphrase doctrine, config, keys; never adopt another identity). Behavioural — asserted by `constraints.md`, backstopped mechanically by R-6, measured by the adversarial corpus. No hook can enforce a behavioural rule. | `evals/adversarial/*.yaml`, pinned present by `test_r14_is_covered_by_the_adversarial_corpus` |
| R-15 | Anti-redundant tool calls — the pre-fill header states what context is already present. | `test_no_redundant_read_fails_when_prefill_was_sufficient` |
| R-16 | The LLM API key value never appears in a log line, trace attribute, exception message, or `raw_json`. | `test_secret_never_logged_in_the_message` |
| R-17 | **A degraded derivation of Nora's own identity is counted, never merely logged.** R-12 is only as strong as `PlatformIdentity.aliases` is complete, so enrichment falling back to `{primary}` silently downgrades a security rule to an equality check. `nora.platform.signal.self_id.derive_identifiers` stays best-effort (it must never block startup) but increments `nora_identity_enrichment_degraded_total{platform,reason}` on every degraded path AND always publishes the `nora_identity_aliases{platform}` gauge — the gauge is the load-bearing half, because a failure counter at 0 cannot distinguish "healthy" from "this code no longer runs". Alert on `nora_identity_aliases == 1`. | `test_degraded_identity_enrichment_is_counted_and_r12_still_holds`; `tests/test_signal_self_id.py` (whole file) |
| R-18 | Somebody else's identifiers never leave in a reply — an E.164 number, a UUID (ACI/PNI), a key-shaped string, or the name of the model she runs on. `constraints.md` forbids relaying them, but that is a request to a model; this is the half that does not depend on it complying. Blocking costs one silent reply, leaking is on every member's device before anyone can react, so it fails closed and counts `nora_egress_blocks_total{kind}`. | `test_a_reply_carrying_someones_phone_number_is_refused` |
| R-20 | **Quoted text shown to the model is the resolved parent ROW's text, or nothing** (`design/11-recall-and-threading.md` §2.4). `build_tag_message` used to render `dataMessage.quote`'s own `text`, attributed to a named author, inside `<tag_message>` — and that text arrives in the QUOTING sender's envelope, verified against nothing, so Alice could construct a quote asserting Bob said anything at all and Nora would relay it back into the room as Bob's words. R-13 escapes it; escaping does not make a forged attribution safe. `nora.agent.prefill._replying_to_block` now resolves `quote.quoted_ts` to a row Nora herself audited and renders THAT row's body under THAT row's label; an unresolvable pointer omits the block entirely rather than falling back to `quote.author_id`, which is a raw platform identifier and so exactly what R-18 keeps off the wire. Fixes Bug A as a side effect: signal-cli's `quote` payload has no `authorName` field at all, so the old `q.get("authorName") or ""` rendered `<replying_to author="">` — "replying to nobody" — on every real message for the whole life of that feature. | `test_a_forged_quote_cannot_put_words_in_someone_elses_mouth` |
| R-21 | **A room-scoped tool resolves names only within the conversation it was bound to, and "not in this room" is indistinguishable from "does not exist".** `group_members`, `lookup_person` and `recall` (`nora.agent.tools`) all resolve inside `nora.agent.room.RoomView.people` — the platform's own membership list for THIS conversation, intersected with `identity.toml` — never against `Persons.index`, which is Nora's address book across every room she is in. The mechanism is **resolve-within-scope, never resolve-then-filter**: the name/alias index `find` runs against is BUILT from this room's members, so a person who is elsewhere is not found-and-rejected, they are absent from the index. Resolve-then-filter would leak membership — and the existence of people in other rooms — through the difference between "no such person" and "not here", which is the 2026-08-06 roster leak in a slower spelling: asked who was in the group Nora listed five people who had never been members, and R-18 saw nothing to block because the leak carried only names (design/12 §3). **By the time text exists the disclosure is already authorised**, so this is enforced at ASSEMBLY. Two consequences worth stating: alias matching is safe HERE while design/10 §1.3 still forbids an alias ever resolving a SENDER (the target set is bounded before any alias is consulted, and `nora.persons.Persons.index` is untouched — `tests/test_persons.py::test_an_alias_never_resolves_a_sender` still holds); and none of the three accepts or returns a platform identifier, so they cannot confirm that an id belongs to a member, and nothing they hand the model is something R-18 would have to block on the way out. **DEC-44 removed `read_peer_card`, this rule's last STRUCTURAL violation** — an unscoped, raw-platform-id lookup that could pull a person's card across conversation boundaries; the room-scoped tools named here are now the whole of the person-lookup surface, and every one of them resolves within scope. | `test_not_being_here_is_indistinguishable_from_not_existing` (`tests/test_room_tools.py`); `tests/unit/test_agent_room.py::test_a_person_in_identity_toml_but_not_in_this_room_is_not_found` |
| R-22 | **A tool that could not answer says so in its RETURN VALUE, and "asked and nothing known" is never the same value as "could not ask".** An empty result that reads as "nobody is here" is a confident lie the group acts on; a raised exception kills the turn. So each of the three room-scoped tools returns a `status` — `ok` / `not_found` / `ambiguous` / `nothing_known` / `unavailable` — and every one is counted (`nora_room_lookups_total{tool,status}`, plus `nora_recall_total{status}` for the Honcho call specifically). **The counter has to live at the call site**, because neither transport can express the distinction: signal-cli returning `[]` for a group Nora is demonstrably in is a real observed state (stale group state on a linked device — CLAUDE.md constraint 3), and Honcho's `representation` answers **200 with an empty string** for a peer it has never heard of rather than 404. This is `peer_card`'s failure written down: it returned `[]` both when Honcho was down and when it knew nothing, so a dead endpoint answered "I know nothing" on every call for the entire life of the deployment and "a missing card is normal" was a true sentence that made it invisible. Consequences: an unreadable roster makes `group_members` `unavailable` and never an empty list; a lookup MISS while the roster is unreadable is `unavailable`, not `not_found` (the scope narrows to the tagger, which is a strict subset of the room and so cannot say what the room does not contain); and a `MemoryStore` that raises lands on `unavailable`, never on empty. | `test_nothing_known_is_distinguishable_from_could_not_ask` (`tests/test_room_tools.py`); `tests/test_honcho_representation.py::test_an_unknown_peer_is_reachable_and_empty_never_unreachable`; `tests/contract/test_roster_source_contract.py` |
| R-23 | **A turn that ran out of budget is never reported as a turn that chose not to speak.** Strands ends a turn that hits one of R-8's caps by yielding an `EventLoopStopEvent` with `stop_reason="limit_total_tokens"` (or `limit_turns` / `limit_output_tokens`) and RETURNING — measured end to end against 1.50.2: no exception, no log line, no warning anywhere in the SDK. `nora/` never read `stop_reason` at all (zero hits), and `LiveAgentRunner.invoke` awaited `factory.invoke(...)` and DISCARDED the `AgentResult` it returned. So `invoke.run` reported `ok=True`, the pipeline found nothing delivered, ⚠️ fired and `nora_silent_failures_total{reason="no_send_call"}` incremented — **byte-identical to the model simply choosing not to call `send_reply`.** The single most likely cause of a silent turn was indistinguishable from the most benign one, and the budget was crossed during model call FIVE of an alleged eight (see R-24), so this was not a corner case. The `AgentResult` is now kept, `stop_reason` is threaded `LiveAgentRunner` → `invoke.run` → `deliver.check_outcome`, every completed invocation is counted `nora_llm_stop_reason_total{reason}` (including `end_turn` — a series that only ever carries `limit_*` cannot say what FRACTION of turns are cut off), and a silent turn whose loop ended on a cap is labelled with that cap instead of `no_send_call`. `check_outcome`'s `stop_reason` has **no default**, so a new call site cannot quietly restore the old behaviour. Only the three BUDGET reasons are relabelled: `end_turn`/`max_tokens` with no reply is still a model that chose not to speak, and swallowing that would trade one blind spot for another. This matters more than R-24's numbers — raising a cap without this just moves the cliff, and it is the difference between finding the next one in an afternoon and it running 61 days like the `--receive-mode` bug. | `test_a_budget_stop_is_distinguishable_from_a_silent_model` (`tests/integration/test_turn_budget_is_legible.py`); `tests/unit/test_engagement_deliver.py::test_a_turn_cut_off_by_a_budget_cap_is_not_labelled_no_send_call`, `::test_an_ordinary_stop_reason_still_counts_no_send_call`, `::test_check_outcome_cannot_be_called_without_a_stop_reason`; `tests/unit/test_engagement_invoke.py::test_the_stop_reason_is_counted_on_every_completed_invocation` |
| R-24 | **No single model call, and no single tool result, may spend the whole turn's budget.** Three violations, all measured 2026-08-06, all previously invisible because a budget that is too small does not fail loudly — it just ends the turn early (which, before R-23, read as the model declining to answer). (1) `build_limits` set `"output_tokens": max_tokens`, making the WHOLE LOOP's output budget equal to ONE call's ceiling; measured single calls emitted 3,993 / 3,987 / 3,974 completion tokens against `max_tokens = 4000`, i.e. 99.8% of it. `output_tokens` is now its own `[llm.limits]` field (12,000 = 6x the per-call ceiling) and `build_limits` has no `max_tokens` parameter left to pass. (2) Real per-call INPUT is 4,952 tokens, paid again on every call, and a measured 6-call turn totalled 32,426-36,748 — so `total_tokens = 24000` was crossed during call FIVE and `turns = 8` was unreachable dead config. `total_tokens = 64000` gives ~2x headroom and lets `turns` bind first, which is the cap an operator can reason about because a turn is a round trip and a token is not. `max_tokens` drops 4000 → 2000: the largest LEGITIMATE completion measured was 371 tokens, so that is 5.4x headroom, and it no longer doubles as the loop budget. (3) `read_buffer`'s `limit` is the one number in `nora.agent.tools` the MODEL invents on every call and nothing downstream bounds it, so the clamp is the only thing between it and the turn: `read_buffer(200)` cost 6,592 tokens (vs `read_buffer(40)` 1,368, `recall` 151, `lookup_person` 112) and is re-charged on every later call of the loop, so at `budget: 2` two calls were 13,184. Clamped 200 → 60, default 40 → 25. The ceiling is a LITERAL in the docstring rather than an interpolation because the docstring IS the model-facing schema — an f-string there would leave `__doc__` empty and Strands would derive no description at all — so a test pins the literal to `READ_BUFFER_MAX` instead. Asserted against **both** config files: `config.toml` is what this deployment runs and `config.example.toml` is what a fresh install gets and what `tests/test_config.py` loads. | `test_no_single_call_can_spend_the_whole_turn_budget` (`tests/test_turn_budget.py`, parametrised over both config files); `::test_both_config_files_declare_the_same_budget`; `::test_the_read_buffer_clamp_is_the_only_bound_on_a_model_supplied_size`; `::test_the_model_is_told_the_same_ceiling_the_clamp_enforces`; `tests/test_factory.py::test_build_limits_takes_the_loop_budget_and_never_the_per_call_ceiling`; `tests/test_tools.py::test_read_buffer_clamps_the_top_end_to_the_declared_ceiling` |
| R-27 | **An empty answer from one operator is never reported as "there is no such thing" until the thing has been shown not to exist.** `transit`'s three states are `ok` / `nothing_found` / `unavailable`, and the split inside `nothing_found` is the rule: a route some operator's CATALOGUE lists and a route no catalogue lists get different sentences, and a catalogue that could not be read makes the answer `unavailable` rather than a claim about the route. Measured 2026-08-06, and this is why it is a correctness requirement rather than a nicety: `getSchedule` answers `200` with `status: "0"`, `caseNumber: 1` and `busStop: []` for `ZZZ999`, **and returned the byte-identical envelope for eleven real published MTR routes at 23:15** (K12, K14, K51A, K52A, K52P, K53S, K58, K65A, K74, K75S, K76S) simply because they had stopped for the night. Nothing in an ETA response separates the two, and the four fields that look as though they might — `caseNumber`, `routeStatus`, `routeColour`, `routeStatusRemarkTitle` — are every one of them marked *Deprecated* in MTR's own Data Dictionary v1.19. Without the catalogue check 「而家冇車」 is said about a bus that has never existed, confidently, in a sentence indistinguishable from a true one. See DEC-40. | `test_an_unknown_route_is_never_reported_as_no_service` |
| R-27b | ...and the same rule on the PLACE axis: «that place exists but this route does not go there» (`not_on_route`) is a useful, checkable sentence — 「K73 唔經天水圍站」 — and is never collapsed into «no stop by that name» (`not_found`), which only a LOADED stop dataset entitles anybody to say; stop data that could not be read makes the place answer `unavailable`, never a claim either way. See DEC-41. | `test_a_place_the_route_does_not_serve_is_never_no_such_place` |
| R-28 | **The OUTBOUND egress boundary: a person's name or resolved position in a routing argument is a hard block, refused before any HTTP call.** R-18 guards REPLIES; a `route` query inherently contains TWO locations and they LEAVE THE HOST to Google, which R-18 never saw. 「由陳生屋企去我公司」 must never resolve a named person to a home address and send it — design/14 §2.2 is explicit that there is no benign reading. Two things are screened out of `origin`/`destination` at the tool seam (`nora.agent.tools.route`) before `RouteClient` is ever called: an IDENTIFIER (phone/ACI/key — `scan_identifiers`, the shared floor) and a PERSON'S NAME OR NICKNAME (substring against the WHOLE `identity.toml` address book, not just this room — 陳生 need not be a group member for the leak to be real — after `Room`'s normalisation, ≥2 chars). A blocked query returns a legible `refused` and reaches nothing. This is the UPPER half of §2.2; the LOWER half is that the resolver (`nora.infra.routes.places`) consults no store that knows a human and so cannot turn a name into a location even if one slipped past. Neither half is sufficient alone. design/14 §2.3 reserved R-27/R-28 for grounding; R-27 went to transit, so this is R-28, and search/fetch take the next free number when they ship. `place_info` carries the identical screen (`nora.agent.tools.place_info`, shipped under DEC-63) — this row's own citation gap, corrected 2026-08-14 alongside DEC-100. See DEC-46. | `test_a_route_naming_a_person_is_refused_before_egress`, `test_place_info_tool_refuses_a_person` |
| R-29 | **A tag for a conversation already handling one is QUEUED, not dropped.** Admission used to DROP a second concurrent tag (`nora_tags_dropped_total{reason="in_flight"}`): three people tagging at once meant person 1 got a reply and persons 2 and 3 got silence — counted, but from inside the room indistinguishable from being ignored, R-5's failure shape pointed at people. Now a busy conversation gets a per-conversation FIFO queue (`nora.engagement.queue.ConversationQueues`), drained one turn at a time as each in-flight turn's `finally` releases R-10's lock and dispatches the next. **Serialisation is correctness, not politeness**: each turn's context is assembled at DEQUEUE time, so tag 2 sees tag 1's reply already in the transcript — two concurrent same-conversation turns would each reason from a room missing the other's reply and could re-answer or contradict. The queue feeds R-10's single slot in order; it never widens it, and DIFFERENT conversations still run concurrently. **Bounded against a flood, not against waiting**: `engagement.queue.max_depth` (default 16) sheds only the newest arrival past the bound (`nora_tags_dropped_total{reason="queue_full"}`, its own counter distinct from debounce); the old `reason="in_flight"` drop is gone. **No staleness rule** — elapsed time is a weak proxy for "the group moved on", and dropping a tag someone still waits on is the exact failure this removes. Composes with R-9 (a debounced tag never enqueues) and R-10 (the lock still guards the single in-flight turn; the queue is a THIRD per-conversation structure). Per-CONVERSATION state only: a `deque` per active conversation, deleted the moment its last waiting tag is dequeued, so nothing is retained per-tag (`tests/architecture/test_no_retained_per_tag_state.py` still holds). The 👀 progress reaction now fires at ADMISSION for a queued tag too — see DEC-48. See DEC-48. | `test_three_tags_for_one_conversation_are_answered_in_fifo_order` |
| R-25 | **A tag that delivered SOME of its messages and then stopped early is counted as a partial reply, never as a complete one.** R-23 made a SILENT turn's cause legible; a turn cut off between message 2 and message 4 is not silent — `delivered` is True, `check_outcome` returns True, the indicator resolves ✅, Honcho is fed, and `nora_silent_failures_total` never moves. The group reads half a thought and, before this rule, no series anywhere said so. `nora_messages_per_tag` cannot answer it either: once the histogram legitimately spans 1–4 a 2 is a 2 whether she meant to stop there or was stopped. `nora_partial_replies_total{reason, platform}` reuses Strands' `limit_*` vocabulary where the loop reported one (so it joins against `nora_llm_stop_reason_total`) and adds `invocation_failed` for a timeout or `llm_error`, which return no stop reason at all, and `pipeline_error` for the outer `BaseException`. Counted in `EngagementPipeline._run_admitted`'s `finally`, the one point every exit path converges on. **And in `FirePath._turn`'s, since the heartbeat shipped:** the send cap is 3 on that path too, so a reminder can half-arrive — and there it is worse, because §8.2's at-most-once means the rest of the thought is never coming. The fire path decides `completed` at the invocation rather than at the end of the block, since a fire turn that timed out AFTER delivering still answers `sent` and therefore does not return early the way a tag does. | `test_a_turn_cut_off_between_messages_is_counted_as_partial`; `tests/unit/test_fire_path.py::test_a_fire_turn_cut_off_between_messages_is_counted_as_partial`, `::test_a_timeout_after_the_reminder_went_out_is_a_partial_delivery` |
| R-29 | **A skill source is operator configuration only; a message can name a skill the operator shipped but can never introduce a new one.** design/12 §2's load-bearing boundary is 3-over-4 as a TRUST boundary, and its skill corollary is that item 6 (message content, untrusted) must never reach item 5 (a skill's trusted, operator-authored instructions): "if a message could name a skill to activate, the ordering collapses". Enforced structurally by `nora.skills.loader.SkillStore`, not by inspection. The ONLY input to loading is `self._dir`, a path the composition root fixes from operator config — no method takes skill CONTENT or a SOURCE as an argument (`test_the_store_exposes_no_way_to_add_a_skill_from_content`). `resolve(name)` is a pure lookup against the already-loaded set: it never reads the filesystem and never treats its argument as a path, a URL or an inline `---` blob (a name-shape pre-check refuses all three before the lookup runs), so the most a message can do is name a skill the operator already shipped — the intended activation (design/12 §4) — and it can never name a NEW one into existence. This is design/14 §2.1's "no path from retrieved text to skill activation" one turn earlier, at the source rather than the reply. The probe reverts `resolve` to the vended `strands...Skill.from_file`/`from_url` shape (load from the argument) and watches a valid skill sitting OUTSIDE the operator directory load — the exact violation. See DEC-48. | `test_a_message_can_name_a_skill_but_never_load_one` (`tests/test_skill_loader.py`) |
| R-33 | **A stored image is described only from THIS conversation's custody, under the vision call's OWN timeout and byte ceiling, and the model-written description is untrusted egress screened on every serve.** `media_fetch` (DEC-56) is the READ half of media custody. Three invariants, each reverting to a real failure. (1) **Scope (R-21):** the handle resolves against `bound_conversation_id`'s own `media_json` only, over a window ≥ prefill + `read_buffer`'s reach — a handle from another conversation is un-resolvable (`nothing_found`), never resolved-then-refused; two in-scope blobs sharing the 6-hex prefix are `ambiguous` (24 bits collide), never pick-first; a blob NO describer reads is `not_an_image` (amended by DEC-114: a describable modality with no describer configured is `unavailable`, not `not_an_image`) BEFORE any MiMo call. (2) **The blocker (design/14, A8/R-23):** the whole agent loop runs inside one `asyncio.wait_for(llm.timeout_seconds)`, so a slow MiMo *reasoning* call would overrun it as the loop's silent `invocation_timeout` (R-5). The vision call gets its OWN timeout DERIVED from `llm.timeout_seconds` (`derive_vision_timeout` = `min(configured, loop × 0.5)`, provably shorter, the way `build_retry_policy` derives its backoff) AND a `vision_max_bytes` ceiling far below `media.max_bytes` — over either, this tool's `unavailable`, never the loop's timeout. `store.get` returning None (absent) OR raising `InvalidTag`/`ValueError` (corrupt / wrong key on the day `MEDIA_ENCRYPTION_KEY` rotates) both wrap into `unavailable`; the tool obeys `test_a_tool_never_raises_into_the_agent_loop`. (3) **Untrusted egress (design/14 §2.1):** the description is text a vision model wrote about bytes a group member sent, cached by content hash and run through `scan_identifiers` on EVERY serve (never once at store time, so a cache hit cannot bypass it), dropped-with-a-note on a hit — the shape `about` uses. **Honest caveat:** `scan_identifiers` catches an ACI/UUID/key/`+`E.164 but NOT a bare local number (`9000 0101`), a residual risk EQUAL to R-18's, stated rather than papered over. Inert-by-config behind `[media] enabled` + key + a reachable OmniRoute client (any absent → `unavailable`). design/14 §2.3 reserved R-27/R-28 for grounding; R-27/R-28 went to transit/route and the presence pair took R-31/R-32, so this is R-33 (R-26 stays reserved by `design/proposed/18`). See DEC-56. | `test_a_stored_image_is_read_only_within_scope_and_screened_on_serve` (`tests/test_media_fetch.py`), and the timeout/byte-ceiling half in `tests/test_vision.py::test_the_vision_timeout_is_always_shorter_than_the_loop_budget` |
| R-34 | **RETIRED by DEC-73 (2026-08-08): `web_fetch`/Firecrawl removed — no fetch, no fence, no rule. Historical text below.** **`fetch` may open only a URL a `search` returned this turn — defence in depth, NOT the SSRF guarantee.** The two web verbs are the only grounding whose OUTBOUND call (a self-hosted fetcher against a model-chosen URL) is an SSRF surface: SearXNG, Honcho, signal-cli (`:18080`), OmniRoute and Firecrawl all listen on localhost, so a model-invented URL makes Firecrawl a pivot into Nora's internals. The PRIMARY control is Firecrawl's network-isolated egress (design/22 §0/§5, a deployment change — the operator ship gate, not this build), because Firecrawl follows redirects and re-resolves DNS itself, so anything Nora checks pre-call is bypassable at the connection Firecrawl actually makes. What Nora's own code enforces is defence in depth: (a) a per-tag `allowed_urls` fence — `fetch` refuses any URL not returned by a `search` this turn, so an invented URL reaches Firecrawl through nothing (`nora.agent.tools`); (b) an `is_global` scheme/host precheck resolved through `getaddrinfo`, rejecting non-http(s) and any host any of whose resolved addresses is not `is_global` (loopback/RFC1918/CGNAT/link-local/ULA/reserved/unspecified, and numeric/hex/IPv4-mapped encodings and rebind DNS names all normalise before the check — `nora.infra.search`). See DEC-57. Next free number after the presence pair (R-31/R-32) and media_fetch (R-33); design/22 §6 wrote R-32 provisionally, reconciled to R-34 at merge. **Live since 2026-08-08 (DEC-69): the PRIMARY control (`egress.sh` packet-layer isolation) is NOT persistently deployed — its rules reopen after every `docker compose up` — so this defence-in-depth is the WHOLE live SSRF control, not the backstop it was designed to be. Operator-accepted residual.** | `test_fetch_refuses_a_url_no_search_returned` (`tests/test_search.py`) |
| R-35 | **A saved place resolves only inside a room that is a verified SUBSET of the circle — and a room that is not falls THROUGH, silently, never refusing.** *Backfilled into this table 2026-08-08; the rule and its test have been live since DEC-60.* The deliberate inverse of R-31's loud refusal: there the subject is a person's live position and silence would lie, here the dictionary entry's EXISTENCE is itself the secret, so a refusal would teach an outsider that a guarded name exists. | `test_a_non_subset_room_resolves_no_saved_place` |
| R-36 | **A reminder never fires early.** The tick's batch is `due_at <= now` and nothing else: no rounding, no tolerance window, no catch-up sweep that treats "nearly due" as due. First of the reminder rules because it is the one a scheduler gets wrong QUIETLY — a message that arrives before the thing it is about reads as Nora being confused, not as a clock bug, and nobody files it. A row the tick cannot run (slot held, policy revoked) stays PENDING or resolves FAILED; it never moves forward in time. | `test_a_row_not_yet_due_does_not_fire` |
| R-37 | **A row fires once, ever.** Taking a row is a COMPARE-AND-SET on `state = 'pending'` inside the statement (`ReminderStore.claim`), not a read-then-write. `due()` is a query rather than a claim — the batch it returns is a snapshot of a past moment, and the tick awaits a turn per conversation before it reaches the later rows — so a cancel landing in that window must win. With `mark` doing the take it did not: `mark` matches on id alone and nothing deletes reminders, so the caller's "already resolved" branch was unreachable and a cancelled row went CANCELLED → FIRING → DONE and was spoken. | `test_claim_is_a_compare_and_set_from_pending` |
| R-37b | **The same rule across a crash, where it is a DECISION rather than a mechanism (at-most-once).** A row left `firing` by a dead process resolves to `failed` and is never re-queued. Nothing links a row to the message it may already have delivered — `messages` has no `tag_id` column, `RecordStore` exposes no query by raw, and `prune_all` could have deleted the row — so re-queueing double-sends anything that crashed after egress. Losing one reminder VISIBLY (`outcome=failed_error`, a counter, a log line) beats saying it twice; "either re-queued or reported" was never a decision. | `test_sweep_resolves_firing_rows_to_failed` |
| R-38 | **Only a mapped `person_id` may create a reminder, and the check is made twice.** `requester_person_id` is the row's owner and the id the fire-time policy re-check runs against, so an unmapped sender has nothing to write and inventing an owner would put unprompted speech into a room under nobody's name. Enforced at `remind()` (`Room.tagger is None` → refused) **and again at fire time**, because `identity.toml` and `policy.toml` are both read once at startup while the horizon is 30 days — the create-time check answered a question about a world that may no longer exist. A guard, not a doctrine line. | `test_remind_refuses_an_unmapped_requester` |
| R-39 | **`remind` refuses on a fire turn — a fire turn may not create reminders.** *Revised 2026-08-10 (DEC-85). The old wording, preserved: “the structural bar on recurrence. v1 has no recurring rows, and this is what keeps it that way”.* The REFUSAL is untouched and so is its test; what changed is what it is for. Recurrence now exists, as a stored property of the row minted inside `claim` (DEC-85) — so the thing this rule bars is no longer recurrence itself but recurrence NOBODY ASKED FOR: without the refusal Nora re-arms her own reminder every time one comes due, an unbounded loop that no `RecurrenceRule.count` bounds because each re-arm is a fresh series. **None of the caps can stop it** — `max_pending_per_requester` counts PENDING rows and the fire has already freed one before the replacement is written, and the new row passes `min_lead_seconds` and `max_horizon_days` trivially. The flag is turn PROVENANCE (`fire_turn`, threaded from `ToolRegistry.build`), which had to be invented for this: a fire turn is a well-formed turn in the same conversation and no tool could otherwise tell the two apart. | `test_remind_refuses_on_a_fire_turn` |
| R-42 | **A version is announced AT MOST ONCE, per room, ever.** Nora's second unprompted-speech occasion (DEC-97), and the failure it exists to prevent is specific: a container crash-looping every four seconds would otherwise post the same upgrade note into a group chat on every boot. The claim is `INSERT … ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` on `version_announcements(version, platform, conversation_id)` — a compare-and-set in ONE statement, `reminders.claim`'s shape (R-37) — and it is taken BEFORE the turn runs, so a row EXISTING bars a second attempt whatever state it holds. That is what makes at-most-once hold across a crash MID-TURN, the case a state check gets wrong, and it is R-37b's decision applied to a second path for R-37b's reason: nothing links a claim to a message that may already have reached the group, so a retry double-posts anything that died after egress. Two states deliberately do NOT claim, so they stay recoverable — an unreadable build stamp and a version with no release note — and a held conversation slot DEFERS without claiming. | `test_a_restart_never_re_announces_the_same_version` (`tests/unit/test_version_announcer.py`, rebuilding everything in-process so only the database survives), plus `::test_a_crash_between_claiming_and_speaking_is_never_re_announced` and `tests/infra/test_announcement_log.py` (the claim itself, against real Postgres) |
| R-46 | **A reply leaves on the platform its tag arrived on, and a platform with no adapter refuses rather than falling back to one that is wired.** Nora held ONE adapter from the day she had one platform, and every outbound path selected it unconditionally — `EngagementPipeline` for the reply and the 👀, `FirePath` for a reminder or an announcement. The moment a second platform existed (`design/discord-integration/`) that stopped being a simplification and became a leak: a Discord tag would resolve Nora's identity against her SIGNAL aliases (so R-12's self-tag check reads an id space the sender shares nothing with) and deliver its answer into a Signal group — a question asked in one room answered in front of a different set of people. Both paths now hold a `platform -> adapter` map keyed by `NormalizedMessage.platform` / `Occasion.platform`. The absent-route case RAISES rather than defaulting: falling back to «whatever adapter is wired» IS the defect, and under R-5 a reply delivered to the wrong group is a wrong answer made public, which is strictly worse than a turn that fails loudly (spawned turns are logged by `BackgroundTasks`; a fire turn's outer guard renders it `failed_error` + `handler_error`). | `test_a_discord_tag_is_answered_on_discord_not_signal` (+ `::test_a_signal_tag_still_goes_to_signal_when_discord_is_also_wired`, `::test_each_platform_resolves_its_own_identity_for_the_self_tag_check`, `::test_an_unwired_platform_refuses_rather_than_picking_an_adapter`) |
| R-47 | **The circle is who may be ASKED ABOUT; the room is only who may be TOLD.** `where_is` resolved its target through `RoomView.find`, so the question "who did they mean?" was scoped to group membership — and a friend who shares her location with Nora, is named in `identity.toml`, and sits in the Life360 circle, but simply is not in THIS group, answered 「呢度冇人叫呢個名」. A false sentence about somebody Nora knows perfectly well (operator, live, 2026-08-14: "i cannot check clara location in signal"). The two questions were always distinct and had been answered by one lookup: R-21/R-31 gate the AUDIENCE (may this room be told anything at all — checked in full, unchanged, BEFORE any name is resolved), while the target is now matched against the circle's own members, unioned with the room's so somebody present but OUTSIDE the circle still resolves and gets the honest «I have no recorded location for them» instead of «who?». Deduped by `person_id`, because a member who is both present and in the circle would otherwise render as `ambiguous` about one person. The operator's rule, stated plainly: **only an identified person may use `where_is`** — which `policy.toml`'s per-room `persons` list already enforces — **and they may ask about anyone.** | `test_a_circle_member_who_is_not_in_the_room_can_still_be_asked_about` (+ `::test_somebody_in_the_room_but_outside_the_circle_still_resolves`) |
| R-49 | **A message may cross into another room only between rooms of the SAME workspace, only at the request of somebody allowed in BOTH, and only under that person's name.** R-2 says a turn's messages all go to the tagging conversation, and that stays true of `send_reply`; `send_to` (DEC-105) is the one gated exception. It matters because every other guardrail here is scoped to ONE conversation — `policy.toml` decides who may speak in a room, R-31 what may be disclosed into it — so a relay reaches past all of them by construction: the words appear in front of an audience that made its own trust decisions. Five refusals, all fail-closed: an **unmapped asker** (a person Nora cannot name cannot be attributed, and an unattributed relay is precisely what attribution prevents); a **room resolved by anything but its operator-written `label`**, refusing rather than guessing when two rooms answer to one name; a **destination in another `workspace`** — the boundary that makes a relay legitimate rather than a leak, since rooms sharing one are one social unit reachable in more than one place while rooms in different ones are different groups of people, however trusted each is alone; an asker **not independently allowed in the destination** (the workspace bounds which rooms may be PAIRED, the persons list decides who may USE the pair — without it, anyone in one room of a workspace could speak into every other, which is a promotion); and text failing the **same egress scan a reply gets** (R-18), because the destination saw neither the asker nor the turn. What arrives ALWAYS names who asked: «dinner at 8» and «Miles: dinner at 8» are different claims to the people reading them and only the second is true, so the attribution is built in the relay rather than left to the model, which could be talked out of it. Send-only, never a bridge. | `test_a_room_in_another_workspace_is_refused` (+ `::test_somebody_not_in_the_destination_cannot_speak_into_it`, `::test_what_arrives_always_says_who_asked`, `::test_an_unmapped_asker_is_refused_rather_than_sent_unattributed`, `tests/test_relay.py`) |
| R-51 | **A sticker description is never invented (sticker spec §6, operator 2026-08-15).** Every way `StickerReader`/`StickerInterpreter` can fail to produce one — the vision call timing out, the format being unreadable (a Discord lottie's `application/json`), the bytes already gone from custody, an empty read-back — leaves the bare `[sticker a3f2c1]` placeholder untouched: never a blank `[sticker a3f2c1: ]` and never a guessed sentence. `StickerReader._read` never caches a failure (a remembered failure would serve itself as an answer on every later sighting), and a description that DOES land still crosses the same egress scan a reply gets (R-18/R-28) before it reaches the body — an identifier in the model's own words costs the description, not the message. Next free number after R-50 (which rode along inside DEC-106's row without one of its own). | `test_r51_a_sticker_description_is_never_invented` (+ `test_an_identifier_in_a_description_costs_the_description_not_the_message`, `test_a_sticker_with_no_reader_wired_keeps_its_handle` in `tests/unit/test_media_custody.py`; `test_an_unfetchable_sticker_yields_no_blob_and_a_reason`, `test_a_failed_interpretation_still_yields_the_blob`, `test_a_reader_raising_mid_ladder_degrades_to_unavailable_and_cleans_up` in `tests/unit/test_sticker_reader.py`) |
| R-53 | **A source that does not mention something has not told you it is false — the SCOPE case, which R-27's three-state discipline never reached (audit F7, 2026-08-20).** `conduct.md`'s «What a source does not say» adds the third outcome beside empty-result and broken-call: a source narrower than the question is `unavailable` («I couldn't check»), never `nothing_found` («there's nothing»), and a silence never overturns something already found with a source behind it. R-27 is enforced on tool RESULTS; nothing enforced it on INFERENCE OVER results, which is where the 2026-08-17 02:16 UTC failure happened — a «Best tower order» article scoped to «all four of the **coloured** towers» was read as denying a black tower, overturning her own correct sourced answer from two minutes earlier. Behavioural, the same shape as R-14/R-41: asserted by `conduct.md`, measured by the corpus, and NOT a currently-passing gate — the corpus is deliberately outside `evals/run.py`'s `SUITES`. **The corpus is graded on its OWN axis (2026-08-20), because the shared rubric's gradient ran BACKWARDS here.** The adversarial gate binds `no_overreach_or_fabrication`, which asks whether a reply claimed MORE than its source supports; reading a silence as a denial claims LESS, and every value in such a reply is real, so the criterion had nothing to catch — measured on `ollamacloud/deepseek-v4-pro:0813` at k=3, temp 0, the failing reply to `scoped_source_silence_stale_window_english` scored PASS 3/3 at 1.0/1.0/0.8 («all stated facts are grounded in the provided source data»). The rubric was not wrong, it was answering a different question, so the fix is a SECOND criterion (`evals/rubric.py::SOURCE_SCOPE`, opted into per case) and NOT a loosening of the first — re-measured after the change, the leaderboard attribution case still FAILs 3/3 at 0.0. Same axis after the change: the denial reply FAILs 2/3, the carry-forward reply PASSes 2/3. | `nora/doctrine/conduct.md`, pinned by `test_conduct_separates_a_source_that_is_silent_from_a_source_that_denies`; `evals/adversarial/source_scope.yaml`, pinned present by `test_r53_is_covered_by_the_source_scope_corpus` and graded by `test_every_source_scope_case_opts_into_the_scope_criterion` |
| R-54 | **A source that does not cover a claim never removes a fact; only a source that addresses it and contradicts it does.** R-53 states this for the model, in `conduct.md`, as prose — and when measured, the prose could not be shown to hold: `evals/adversarial/source_scope.yaml` was, and still is, deliberately held out of `evals/run.py`'s `SUITES` (a measured gap, not a gate), and the shared rubric was found grading it BACKWARDS — `no_overreach_or_fabrication` asks whether a reply claimed MORE than its source supports, so a reply that read a scoped source's silence as a denial and withdrew a correctly-sourced fact scored PASS 3/3 while quoting a denial the source never made. **That grading defect has since been fixed** (`8ffe33b`): `evals/rubric.py::SOURCE_SCOPE` is now a second criterion, opted into per case via `criteria: [source_scope]`, and it binds fail-closed — `evals.judge.case_verdict` fails a case on ANY named criterion's FAIL, so silence-read-as-denial now scores a SCOPE FAIL rather than a pass. The corpus itself stays out of `SUITES` on purpose (`source_scope.yaml`'s own header: "wire it in when it reliably passes, not before") — the fix corrected how a wired-in case would be JUDGED, not whether the doctrine reliably clears it. R-54 is the same rule moved from prose into a SHAPE, DEC-78's move applied to inference over sources: `FactVerdict.not_covered` (`nora/domain/facts.py`) has no reachable `supersedes`, and `contradicted` requires both a source and `addresses_claim` — «the source did not cover it, therefore drop the fact» is unrepresentable at the type, not merely discouraged in the prompt. | `tests/domain/test_facts.py::test_not_covered_cannot_supersede_anything`, `::test_contradicted_requires_a_source_that_addresses_the_claim` |

## DEC-1…DEC-13 — Architecture decisions (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-04-nora-architecture-design.md`)

| ID | Statement | Enforcing test |
|---|---|---|
| DEC-1 | Pipeline + ports, light layering: composable stages behind `Protocol` ports, one composition root, registries from config. | structural — no single test; `nora/ingest/pipeline.py::IngestPipeline` (four stages — audit, identity, memory_feed, gate; parsing moved onto the adapter in the platforms phase, so it is no longer a pipeline stage) and `nora/engagement/pipeline.py::EngagementPipeline` (admission/context/invoke/deliver/feedback) are the pipelines this decision names, `nora/app/composition.py::build_dependencies` is the one composition root that wires them, and `nora/platform/registry.py::AdapterRegistry` is the "registries from config" half, built in the platforms phase |

| DEC-13 | **[built in phase 6 Task 4, recorded 2026-08-05]** Spec §4.5 named four module-level mutable singletons for deletion: `nora/agent/factory.py`'s `_CLIENT` (the process-wide `AsyncOpenAI` client, rebound via `global` inside `create_client`/`close_client`) and `nora/obs/metrics.py`'s `METRICS`/`DECLARED`/`_BUCKETS`. `DECLARED`/`_BUCKETS` were already gone by phase 6 Task 1 (folded into `DECLARED_NAMES`, a frozenset, and `_DEFAULT_BUCKETS`, a plain tuple — both immutable module-level *constants*, which this rule was never about). Task 4 deleted the remaining two: `METRICS = Metrics()` is gone from `nora/obs/metrics.py`; every former default-parameter consumer (`nora.doctrine.loader.DoctrineStore.__init__`, `nora.agent.guards.Deliverer.__init__`, `nora.agent.guards.ReplyGuard.__init__`) now takes `metrics: Metrics` as a required keyword-only parameter, with every call site (production and test) passing one explicitly — `nora.app.composition.build_dependencies` constructs the one production `Metrics()` and threads it to `DoctrineStore` the same way it already did to the engagement pipeline. `_CLIENT` is gone from `nora/agent/factory.py`; `create_client` is now a plain constructor (no memoization, no `global`), called exactly once by `build_dependencies`, whose result is `Dependencies.client` — `nora.__main__.App.shutdown` closes it directly (`await self.deps.client.close()`) instead of calling back into a module-level `close_client()` (also deleted). `tests/conftest.py`'s autouse `_reset_metrics` fixture — which existed only to isolate the `METRICS` module-level name between tests — is deleted outright rather than repurposed: with no singleton left, every test already constructs its own `Metrics()` (or receives one via a builder/fixture), so there is nothing left for an autouse fixture to reset. | `tests/architecture/test_no_module_level_mutable_singletons.py` (two AST checks: no module-level name bound to a call constructing a class defined in the same module; no `global` statement anywhere under `nora/` — both catch the *shape* of `METRICS = Metrics()` / `_CLIENT`'s `global` rebinding, not just those exact names); `tests/test_factory.py` (`create_client` is a plain, non-memoizing constructor); `tests/test_main.py::test_shutdown_closes_the_omniroute_client` (`app.deps.client.is_closed()` after shutdown) |

**On "mypy `--strict`":** `pyproject.toml`'s `[[tool.mypy.overrides]]` for `nora.domain.*`/`nora.config`/`nora.app.*` deliberately hand-expands `--strict`'s flag list instead of setting `strict = true`, working around a confirmed mypy 2.3.0 bug where `strict = true` inside an override section leaks strict mode to every module in the run, not just the matched ones (see the comment above that override in `pyproject.toml`). This is a frozen snapshot of what `--strict` meant as of mypy's pinned version — it must be re-diffed against `--strict`'s actual flag set on every mypy upgrade, or the override silently drifts from what "strict" means in the new version.
| DEC-14 | **Many accounts per platform is the identity baseline** (design/10 §1, §3, §7.4), not an edge case — settled with the operator 2026-08-05 after his two Signal accounts broke the first draft of §3, which modelled many *id forms* of ONE account and had nowhere to put a second registration. One `[[persons.<id>.<platform>]]` block per account; every id in every block flattens into one reverse index, so all of a person's accounts resolve to one `person_id` and therefore one Honcho peer (A2). Splitting them into two `person_id`s would have split his peer card. **Multiplicity lives at the table level, never at the leaf**: a list-valued kind (`phone = [...]`) is refused, because it severs the phone↔ACI pairing within a registration, makes `nora persons pending`'s paste-ready stanza unimplementable for an existing person, and would require widening the leaf type — the one thing stopping `str([...])` from entering the index as a key no sender can present. Second hard break on `identity.toml`, same §7.3 reasoning, with a bespoke error naming the `[[ ]]` form. Two loudness rules guard otherwise-silent authoring errors: an account with no matchable id is refused, and the same id in two of one person's blocks is refused (the copy-paste-and-forget-to-edit mistake, which nothing else catches because both blocks map to the same person so the cross-person check never fires). | `tests/test_persons.py::test_two_accounts_on_one_platform_resolve_to_the_same_person`, `::test_the_same_id_in_two_account_blocks_is_a_startup_failure`, `::test_a_label_is_never_matchable`, `::test_the_retired_single_table_form_names_its_replacement` |
| DEC-15 | **Ingest resolves identity BEFORE the audit write, and counts unresolved senders.** An unmapped sender was previously symptomless: the message was still audited, the gate still ran, and `Persons.display_name` still rendered the sender's platform display name into the prefill, so Nora replied normally and addressed them by name while nothing at all reached Honcho — the display-name fallback actively fabricating the appearance that identity worked. Now `nora_unmapped_senders_total{platform}` counts it and a redacted warning names the likely cause. The resolved `person_id` is persisted on the audit row (`0002_person_id`, nullable, unindexed, declared LAST so a fresh database matches `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN`'s append position). Stage order was swapped rather than adding an UPDATE: A9 permits a second write (it is what `guards.py` does for outbound replies) but `nora.infra.sql.audit` documents that WAL allows one writer and this path DROPS the message on contention — pruning was moved off the write path for that reason, so identity resolution (two dict lookups, no I/O) moved ahead of it instead. Counter and column are not redundant: metrics live only in the process registry and reset on restart, while mapping is startup-only (Q4), so "I fixed identity.toml but never restarted" is a state the file on disk cannot reveal. | `tests/unit/test_ingest_stages.py::test_an_unmapped_sender_is_audited_but_never_fed`, `tests/infra/test_schema_parity.py` |
| DEC-16 | **Adoption is lazy: a person's pre-mapping messages are backfilled into Honcho at the next startup, feed-then-mark.** DEC-15 made an unmapped sender visible and durable (`nora_unmapped_senders_total`, `person_id` NULL on the row) but changed nothing about the memory hole itself: `nora.ingest.stages.memory_feed` bare-returns on `person is None`, and mapping is startup-only (Q4), so every message from before the operator's `identity.toml` edit stayed unfed **forever** — the peer card silently missing the person's entire history up to their adoption. `nora.app.adoption.backfill` reconciles it: scan `RecordStore.unattributed` (person_id IS NULL, `is_nora = 0`, **ordered by `ts`** so the peer's memory reads chronologically, not in insertion order), re-resolve each row's `(platform, sender_id)` against the freshly-loaded `Persons`, feed the message to that person's peer, and only then `RecordStore.set_person_id`. **The order is the decision.** Marking first would retire the row from every future scan the moment a Honcho failure — or a crash — landed between the two, making the hole permanent and silent; feeding first re-feeds on the next restart instead, and at-least-once is the tolerable direction. `person_id` is itself the idempotence marker, so there is no separate ledger. Scheduling follows `prune_all`'s precedent exactly (DEC-15's own reasoning, and `nora.infra.sql.audit`'s docstring): `App.run` spawns it through `BackgroundTasks` after a delay, nothing awaits it, and `set_person_id` writes one row per statement — a backfill that stalled the SSE reader or cost an audit row under WAL's single writer would be a regression, not a feature. Honcho stays best-effort (a raising `MemoryStore` is converted to `False`, a failing sweep is logged and counted, neither can take startup down). Two new counters, `nora_lazy_adoptions_total{platform}` and `nora_adoption_failures_total{platform}`, plus an unconditional INFO line carrying scanned/resolved/fed/marked/failed — a backfill nobody can see having run is the R-5 failure again. | `tests/unit/test_adoption.py` (`::test_a_honcho_failure_leaves_the_row_unmarked_for_the_next_start` is the ordering test; `::test_running_twice_does_not_double_feed_or_double_mark` the idempotence one), `tests/contract/test_audit_store_contract.py` (the `unattributed`/`set_person_id` block, both stores), `tests/test_main.py::test_app_run_spawns_the_lazy_adoption_backfill` (the wiring) |
| DEC-17 | **A tag is acknowledged with a 👀 reaction on the tagging message, and that indicator is always resolved — ✅ when a reply went out, ⚠️ when none did.** Built as `nora.engagement.stages.indicate` + `PlatformAdapter.react()` (`nora.platform.signal.react.SignalReactor` → signal-cli's `sendReaction`; params `groupId`/`emoji`/`targetAuthor`/`targetTimestamp`/`remove`, read off the 0.14.7 man pages, not guessed). **The failure resolution is the decision.** The request was ✅ "whether it succeeds or fails"; a ✅ on a tag that produced no reply asserts a delivery that never happened and leaves the group waiting for a message that is not coming — the inverse of R-5, a false success rather than a leaked error, and worse because a silent failure is at least honestly ambiguous. The two alternatives are this project's own wrong-silence bug in user-facing form: a 👀 left forever makes "thinking" and "broken" the same observation (the 61-day `--receive-mode` outage, relocated into the group), and removing it silently makes "gave up" and "never triggered" the same one. ⚠️ is honest and still discloses nothing R-5 protects: it is not text, it names no cause, and every failure class — timeout, guard veto, leak block, audit failure — is indistinguishable under it (what DEC-4 requires of `INFRASTRUCTURE`/`GUARD_VETO`), so the only bit it adds is one the group can already see, that the reply is missing. ❌ was rejected as reading like an incident report, and a person emoji (the original 🤷) as carrying skin tone, gender and human affect — a shrug reads as dismissiveness, which is a lot of tone for "the model timed out". An intermediate ⚪ was colour-neutral but too easy to scroll past; the operator chose ⚠️ on 2026-08-05 because this marker exists to be NOTICED. **R-2/R-2b/R-3/R-7 are untouched by construction**: a reaction is not a registered tool (so `ReplyGuard`'s injected `budgets` is unchanged and R-7's default-deny still refuses a model that invents one), it never touches `Deliverer` (so the single-attempt latch cannot be consumed or re-armed), its target is `message.target()` and takes no model input at all, and `Deliverer` remains the only caller of `adapter.send()`. **Spawned, never awaited** — `SignalReactor` runs a 10s timeout, so awaiting the 👀 would let a wedged daemon add ten seconds to every tag before the model started; `Indicator.placed` (an `asyncio.Event` set in a `finally`) serialises begin-before-resolve without either being on the reply path. Resolution lives in the pipeline's `finally` so all four failure exits reach it. Every reaction failure is silent and counted as `nora_reactions_total{platform,phase,status}`, with `unsupported` kept distinct from `failed` so a correctly-degrading platform never reads as broken. | `tests/unit/test_engagement_indicate.py::test_a_turn_that_produced_no_reply_resolves_to_a_caution_never_a_check_mark` (the decision), `tests/unit/test_engagement_pipeline.py::test_reactions_do_not_consume_the_reply_budget_so_r2_still_allows_one_reply`, `::test_a_reaction_failure_never_prevents_the_reply`, `::test_a_platform_that_cannot_react_still_replies_normally`, `::test_the_reply_does_not_wait_for_the_reaction`, `tests/contract/test_reaction_contract.py` (both `react()` implementations), `tests/test_signal_react.py` (the wire shape) |
| DEC-18 | **Two allowlists in a third file, deny by default, enforced in two different places.** `policy.toml` is separate from `config.toml` (deployment) and `identity.toml` (who people are) because policy changes when TRUST changes — DEC-8's argument, extended; an allowlist buried among port numbers is an allowlist nobody reviews. It names people by `person_id` only, so adding an account to `identity.toml` grants it access everywhere that person already has it, and — worth stating because it is learned by surprise otherwise — allowlisting `miles` admits **every** account he holds (DEC-14). **The two halves of the grant predicate are enforced at different points on purpose.** The ROOM is checked BEFORE the audit write (`nora/ingest/stages/scope.py`), so a conversation the operator never allowed is not recorded at all — anyone can add Nora to a group, and deny-by-default would be hollow if the refusal still wrote every message to disk; discovery does not depend on it, since `nora groups` reads `listGroups` directly. The SENDER is checked at the point of answering, AFTER the audit, because in an allowed room an unrecognised sender is exactly what `nora persons pending` must be able to see. `persons = "*"` admits an unmapped sender (the room is the trust boundary, and D6 already settled that they are answered but never remembered); an explicit list excludes them, since `None` matches no `person_id`. Nora **refuses to start** on a missing file, an empty allowlist, `default = "allow"`, an unknown key (`extra="forbid"` — `persosn = [...]` would otherwise admit nobody, quietly), or a conversation id listed twice, because under R-5 a silently-denied Nora is indistinguishable from a working one in a quiet group. Both refusals are counted. | `tests/test_policy.py` (the grant predicate and the five refuse-to-start cases), `tests/unit/test_ingest_scope.py` (the room gate), `tests/unit/test_ingest_pipeline.py::test_a_denied_conversation_is_never_recorded`, `tests/unit/test_ingest_pipeline.py::test_a_denied_sender_is_recorded_but_not_answered` |
| DEC-19 | **Nora's own ids are typed `Identifier`s, from the same model as everybody else's, and are derived over JSON-RPC — not from `/api/v1/accounts`, which signal-cli has never served.** Amends DEC-9's mechanism. `PlatformIdentity` moves from `nora.domain.ports` to `nora.domain.identity` (next to `Identifier`, and off ports.py's 300-line cap) and gains `identifiers: frozenset[Identifier]`; `aliases` is now DERIVED from it (`{i.value} | {primary}`), so the flat matchable set can never disagree with the typed one — the same flattening `nora.persons.load_persons` does for `identity.toml`, which is what makes "is this Nora?" and "which person is this?" one matching rule (design/10 §5). Derivation is `nora.platform.signal.self_id.derive_identifiers`: one batched POST to `/api/v1/rpc` running `listContacts` (local read → phone, **ACI**, username) and `getUserStatus` (→ **PNI**, which is NOT the ACI — wiring only that call would have collected the wrong UUID and still looked fixed). `listAccounts` is multi-account-mode only (`-32601` here), so `derive_account` keeps no discovery path and `platforms.signal.account` stays load-bearing. | `tests/test_signal_self_id.py`; `tests/test_signal_adapter.py::test_resolve_identity_publishes_typed_identifiers_not_just_strings`; `tests/test_signal_account.py::test_the_failure_message_names_the_config_key_that_is_the_real_fix` |
| DEC-20 | **The operator control plane is two read-only commands that only ever SUGGEST.** `nora groups` prints every group's name beside the canonical `conversation_id` and whether `policy.toml` allows it — the answer to "I cannot tell which group id belongs to which group, and there is no control plane for me". `nora persons pending` lists senders that map to no person, **correlated into ACCOUNTS rather than raw ids**: under sealed sender one account arrives as a phone number on one message and an ACI on another, so filing them separately would invite two `[[persons.<id>.signal]]` blocks for one registration — exactly the duplicate DEC-14 makes a startup error. Correlation unions the audit envelopes with `listContacts` **and** the `listGroups` member rosters, which is load-bearing rather than belt-and-braces: on this deployment only 3 of 83 contacts carry both a number and a uuid, while every group member does, so `listContacts` alone would fail for almost every sender. Both commands emit paste-ready stanzas and **write nothing** — no file, no daemon call that mutates, and a `mode=ro` + `PRAGMA query_only` SQLite connection (the wal/shm sidecars a read materialises are the documented exception; `immutable=1` would return torn reads while Nora is running). D6 is preserved exactly: the operator adopts by hand-editing `identity.toml` and restarting, and DEC-16 backfills from there. Both work with the container stopped and need no LLM credential — `config.load(..., require_secrets=False)` is scoped to the control plane, and a test asserts the daemon still refuses to start without one. | `tests/test_cli_control_plane.py` (correlation, read-only-ness, and the no-credential path), `tests/test_signal_directory.py` (the RPC read-method allowlist) |
| DEC-21 | **An @-mention triggers Nora, and `tool_choice="required"` is what lets her answer.** Two defects found on the first real tag, both of which made her look broken in different ways. (1) A Signal @-mention — the way anyone would actually tag her — is not text: the body carries `U+FFFC` (OBJECT REPLACEMENT CHARACTER) where the name appears and the mentioned ids ride in a parallel `mentions` array, so `is_tagged`'s substring match could never see one. Tagging her the obvious way produced ingest, attribution, and total silence. `MentionPolicy` matches against her own alias set — the same one R-12 uses — which only became trustworthy when DEC-19 fixed alias derivation, since the mention carries her ACI and nothing else. `CompositePolicy` runs trigger text first, so an explicit `nora.000` can never be blocked by a later policy. A structural mention is a STRONGER signal than text, not a weaker one: R-11 exists to separate tagging intent from string presence, and a mention cannot be quoted in a code span, smuggled through zero-width characters, or produced by accident. (2) `deepseek-v4-flash` does not emit `tool_calls` on its own — given an explicit tool definition and a system prompt demanding one it answers in prose with `finish_reason="stop"`, and `tool_choice="auto"` changes nothing. Since `send_reply` IS a tool, she was structurally mute. `tool_choice="required"` in the model params produces the call every time and constrains her to call SOME tool, leaving `read_buffer`/`read_peer_card` reachable and R-7's default-deny untouched. Verified end to end on the live group: an @-mention now produces a delivered reply. | `tests/test_tag.py` (the mention policy and composite ordering), `tests/test_signal_parser.py::test_mentions_are_extracted_in_every_id_form` |
| DEC-23 | **Threading is one nullable column, resolved in memory, rendered as one arrow — and the platform's own timestamp is what makes it work** (`design/11-recall-and-threading.md` §2.2–2.6). `messages.quoted_ts` stores `dataMessage.quote.id`, written in the SAME INSERT the parser's value arrives on (DEC-15's reason: WAL permits one writer and the ingest path drops the message on contention). ONE column, not two: the parent row's own `sender_id`/`person_id` is authoritative while `quote.author` is supplied by whoever built the quote, so storing both would be two sources of truth and the untrustworthy one is the one that would get rendered — store the pointer, derive the attribution. Rejected: parsing `raw_json` on read (free-looking, but Nora's own rows carry `{tag_id, source, doctrine_sha256}` and no envelope at all, and it would push `dataMessage.quote` knowledge into platform-neutral `nora/engagement`), a `threads` table, transitive chains, reaction and mention edges. Rendering is `[Tyler ↳ Grant]: 你屋企` — ~3 tokens, only threaded lines pay, and the name after ↳ is BYTE-IDENTICAL to the parent's own line label (`_label`) so the model joins by literal match. An unresolvable parent renders a PLAIN line, never `↳ ?`: teaching the model a symbol for "unknown" invites it to emit one. **What makes any of it work for Nora herself** is Bug B: `Deliverer` stamped `int(time.time()*1000)` locally while Signal assigns its own sent-timestamp, and the quote points at Signal's — measured 8 ms apart on live data, close enough to look right and never equal, so an exact join silently resolved every sender in the room except her, and a reply TO Nora is the most common reply in a group where Nora is tagged. `SignalSender.send` now reads `result.timestamp` off the JSON-RPC body it used to discard (`{"timestamp": <long>, "results": [...]}`, read out of signal-cli 0.14.7's `SendMessageResultUtils.outputResult` bytecode), and `Deliverer` corrects the row inside the `set_delivery_outcome` call it already made — NO new write, so single-writer and A9's record-first ordering both stand. `read_buffer` gains a `reply_to` key resolved only within the rows it already returns, because a transcript that teaches `↳` beside a tool that omits it teaches the model that a missing arrow means "not a reply". | `test_a_forged_quote_cannot_put_words_in_someone_elses_mouth` (R-20), `tests/infra/test_alembic.py::test_0003_adds_quoted_ts_without_disturbing_the_ts_desc_index`, `tests/contract/test_audit_store_contract.py` (`quoted_ts` + `set_delivery_outcome`'s `ts`, both stores), `tests/test_guards_deliverer.py::test_noras_row_carries_the_platform_timestamp_so_a_reply_can_join_it`, `tests/test_prefill.py::test_the_arrow_name_is_the_literal_label_the_parents_own_line_uses` |
| DEC-24 | **A tool never asks the model for a value the tool does not read.** `read_buffer(conversation_id, conversation_type, limit)` and `send_reply(conversation_id, conversation_type, text)` required two conversation arguments each and read neither: DEC-21 had already made R-3 hold BY CONSTRUCTION, so `read_buffer` served the closure's bound conversation and `send_reply` went to `Deliverer`'s bound target no matter what arrived. Strands derives the model-facing schema from the callable (`FunctionToolMetadata.extract_metadata` — the docstring above `Args:` becomes the description, each annotated parameter becomes one `inputSchema` property), so a parameter that exists is a value the model is FORCED to invent on every single call. On the first real tag `deepseek-v4-flash` invented the wrong group id three times, R-3 cancelled each call, and the turn died `no_send_call` — the cost of the parameters was a mute Nora; the benefit was zero by construction. The signatures are now `read_buffer(limit=40)` and `send_reply(text)`. **The scope counter is deliberately KEPT, and that is the decision.** R-17's lesson is that a counter which cannot increment is indistinguishable from deleted code, and the naive reading here is that `nora_tool_scope_violations_total` becomes exactly that. It does not: `BeforeToolCallEvent` fires on the model's RAW `tool_use["input"]`, and Strands validates AFTERWARDS through a pydantic model built from the signature, which ignores undeclared properties (verified against 1.50.2). So a model that still names a conversation reaches the guard, is counted, and has the argument dropped before the tool body runs — the observation survives, and it is worth having, because after this change the group id is not in the prompt at all and one appearing is invented rather than echoed. Two changes make it honest: the test is `is not None` rather than `!=` (absence is now the normal case, and comparing a missing key would peg the counter at one per tool call — dead in the other direction), and it applies to EVERY tool rather than the two formerly-scoped ones, since the argument is out-of-schema everywhere.
